Conservative Analyst: I've listened to five rounds of this debate, and I want to address both of my colleagues' final arguments with precision, because what's happened in this last exchange is that both of them have made errors that, while sophisticated, ultimately reinforce why patience is the correct approach.

Let me start with the aggressive analyst's most clever argument, because it's the one that could trip up a trader who isn't paying close attention. They take the neutral analyst's marginal EV calculation of negative 0.19% of total capital and argue that since the cost is "practically free," you should maximize the option rather than minimize it. Nineteen basis points is less than a bid-ask spread, less than a day of NVDA volatility, so why not deploy the full 20%?

Here's the fundamental error in this reasoning. The aggressive analyst is treating expected value as if it's the cost you pay. It's not. Expected value is the probability-weighted average of all possible outcomes. The actual cost you pay in any given scenario is not the expected value — it's the outcome that actually materializes. The aggressive analyst's own math, using the neutral analyst's probability framework, shows a 2.5% crash scenario where the starter tranche loses 2.16% of total capital. That's not nineteen basis points. That's two hundred sixteen basis points. The aggressive analyst is telling you the ticket is free because the average price across all possible tickets is nineteen basis points. But you don't pay the average. You pay the price on the ticket you actually draw. And in the 2.5% scenario, that price is 2.16% of total capital — more than eleven times the "trivial" expected value the aggressive analyst is using to justify deployment.

This is the core intellectual error running through the aggressive analyst's entire case. They consistently treat expected values as if they're costs, and they treat variance as if it's irrelevant. But risk management IS the management of variance. A bet with near-zero expected value and high variance is not "practically free." It's a gamble where the average outcome is roughly neutral but the actual outcomes range from modestly positive to significantly negative. The aggressive analyst's framework would justify any bet with a near-zero expected value regardless of the variance, which is a prescription for eventual ruin, not for risk management.

Now, the neutral analyst's calculation deserves credit for being the most rigorous quantitative contribution to this debate. They ran the marginal value calculation in the correct framework — the starter as the first leg of a cost-averaging strategy — and arrived at approximately negative 0.19%. But I want to challenge several assumptions embedded in that calculation that make it more favorable to deployment than the evidence warrants.

First, the neutral analyst assumes the stock recovers to $224 in the pullback scenario, making the blended position's loss temporary. But this assumption is doing enormous work in the calculation. If the stock pulls back to $210 and doesn't recover to $224 within the trader's holding period — perhaps because earnings disappoint, perhaps because the HBM shortage is confirmed, perhaps because the market pulls back and NVDA's 2.215 beta amplifies the move — then the starter tranche's loss is not partially recovered. It's a permanent drag on the blended position. The neutral analyst's recovery assumption is an optimistic input disguised as a neutral one. If you use a more conservative recovery assumption — say the stock recovers to $218 instead of $224 — the marginal cost in the pullback scenario increases from approximately 0.5% to approximately 0.7% of total capital, and the weighted marginal value shifts from negative 0.19% to approximately negative 0.29%. Small changes in assumptions produce meaningful changes in the conclusion when the expected value is near zero, which is exactly why near-zero EV calculations should not drive deployment decisions.

Second, the neutral analyst assigns 17.5% probability to the breakout scenario. I'd argue this is generous. The trader's own framework assigns 15-20%, and the aggressive analyst has spent this entire debate trying to convince us the probability is higher. But let's look at what the technical data actually says. The stock is pressing the upper Bollinger Band after an 18% rally in seven sessions. The TD-9 daily count is at negative 6, three sessions from completion. Price is 8.7% above the 50-SMA — the widest spread in the lookback period. Prior rally attempts at lower levels failed. The neutral analyst's 17.5% is a midpoint of a range that was established before accounting for the multi-timeframe TD-9 stacking and the specific velocity of this rally. A probability of 12-15% is more defensible given the technical evidence, and at 13.5% midpoint, the breakout contribution to the EV calculation drops meaningfully.

Third, the neutral analyst assigns only 2.5% probability to the crash scenario. With a 2.215 beta, a normal 5% market pullback takes NVDA to the stop at $199.78. A 5% market pullback within the next two to three weeks is not a crash. It's ordinary volatility. The 2.5% probability assignment significantly understates the probability of hitting the stop through normal market volatility amplified by NVDA's beta. A more realistic probability for the stop being triggered — through any combination of market pullback, TD-9 completion, Bollinger Band rejection, or supply chain news — is probably 8-12%, not 2.5%. At 10% probability, the crash scenario's contribution to the EV calculation increases from negative 0.054% to negative 0.216%, which alone shifts the total marginal value from negative 0.19% to negative 0.35%.

When you adjust these three assumptions — recovery to $218 instead of $224, breakout probability at 13.5% instead of 17.5%, stop-hit probability at 10% instead of 2.5% — the marginal value calculation shifts from approximately negative 0.19% to approximately negative 0.40% to negative 0.50% of total capital. I'm not claiming my assumptions are definitively correct. I'm claiming that when the expected value is near zero, small changes in assumptions produce large changes in the sign and magnitude of the result, and the neutral analyst's specific assumptions were systematically favorable to deployment. Near-zero EV calculations are fragile — they're highly sensitive to inputs, which means they provide a poor foundation for deployment decisions. You don't deploy capital on the basis of a calculation where changing one assumption by 5 percentage points flips the sign.

The neutral analyst acknowledged this sensitivity when they said "the probability assignments are debatable" and "possibly slightly positive under different assumptions." But then they proceeded to build a recommendation on this fragile foundation anyway. If the calculation is sensitive enough that debatable assumptions can flip its sign, the calculation doesn't support deployment. It supports waiting for conditions where the EV is clearly positive regardless of reasonable assumption variations.

Now let me address the aggressive analyst's argument that my PEG criticism "proves too much" because no stock could pass a standard requiring the PEG to stay below 1.0 under every adverse scenario simultaneously. This is a mischaracterization of my position, and I think the aggressive analyst knows it.

I'm not requiring the PEG to stay below 1.0 under a multi-variable catastrophe. I'm making a much simpler point: the trader's plan assumed a bear-case PEG of 0.63. The aggressive analyst's own calculation showed the normalized PEG is 0.80-0.85. The gap between 0.63 and 0.80-0.85 represents a reduction in the margin of safety that the plan was built on. The plan's deployment sizing — 20% at $224 — was calibrated to a margin of safety that doesn't exist. The appropriate response to discovering that your margin of safety is thinner than assumed is not to argue that it's still technically below 1.0. It's to recalibrate the deployment to the actual margin of safety, not the assumed one.

The aggressive analyst says the 0.80-0.85 PEG "already uses conservative inputs." But does it? The aggressive analyst calculated this PEG using annualized operating income from a single quarter — Q1 FY2027, which was the strongest quarter in NVDA's history. Annualizing the best quarter ever is not conservative. It's optimistic by definition. A genuinely conservative approach would use a run-rate that accounts for the possibility that Q1 was a peak — perhaps the average of the last four quarters' operating income, which would be lower. And the forward growth rate the aggressive analyst used — even their "conservative" assumption — was based on hyperscaler capex commitments that are gross spending numbers, not NVDA-specific revenue commitments. The 0.80-0.85 PEG is not a conservative estimate. It's a mid-cycle estimate presented as conservative. The genuinely conservative PEG — using trailing operating income rather than peak-quarter annualization, and a forward growth rate that accounts for law-of-large-numbers deceleration — is probably 0.90-1.0. At that level, the margin of safety has essentially disappeared, and the undervaluation thesis rests entirely on continued hypergrowth, which is an assumption, not a fact.

The aggressive analyst accuses me of tautology — "if things go badly, the investment will do badly" — but they're engaging in their own tautology: "if things continue as they are, the investment will do well." Both statements are true. Both are unhelpful. The question is the probability of each, and the aggressive analyst systematically overweights the continuation scenario while dismissing the deterioration scenario as a tail event not worth pricing.

On the supply chain risk — the aggressive analyst says the stop at $199.78 is the appropriate response to unconfirmed risks, and that reducing sizing in addition to having a stop is "double-counting." This reveals a misunderstanding of how layered risk management works. The stop and the sizing reduction serve different purposes. The stop limits the loss if the risk materializes. The sizing reduction limits the portfolio's exposure to the risk in the first place. These are not redundant — they're complementary. A stop at $199.78 on a 20% position limits the loss to 2.16% of total capital. A stop at $199.78 on a 0% position limits the loss to 0% of total capital. The sizing reduction reduces the amount of capital that the stop has to protect. That's not double-counting. That's defense in depth. The aggressive analyst's argument would suggest that having both a seatbelt and an airbag is double-counting safety, when in fact they address different types of impact.

The neutral analyst's position on this — that unconfirmed risks create wider distributions that justify modestly more conservative sizing — is more defensible than the aggressive analyst's binary framework. But the neutral analyst's proposed adjustment, reducing from 20% to 15%, attributes the entire reduction to a combination of factors without disaggregating them. If the PEG issue accounts for some of the reduction and the supply chain risk accounts for some, then the supply chain risk's contribution to the sizing reduction is modest. That's probably appropriate for a single unconfirmed retail post. But the neutral analyst doesn't account for the fact that the supply chain risk doesn't exist in isolation. It exists alongside a technical setup that's at its most extended point in the lookback period, a TD-9 countdown three sessions from completion, a PEG margin of safety that's thinner than assumed, and a 2.215 beta in a market where rate expectations could shift hawkish. The neutral analyst evaluates each risk factor independently and assigns each a modest sizing reduction. But the risk factors don't operate independently. They create a risk environment — a context in which any single adverse development is more costly because the position is already vulnerable on multiple fronts. The neutral analyst's independent-risk framework misses this contextual effect.

The aggressive analyst's strongest argument in this final round is their critique of my recoverability framework. They argue that the specific opportunity to buy NVDA at $224 with the current catalyst pipeline is non-replicable, and that treating it as fungible with future opportunities is an analytical error. Let me address this directly because it's the argument most likely to persuade a trader.

The aggressive analyst is partially right. The specific constellation of conditions — three SuperTrend timeframes in uptrend, MACD expanding, $2B AI deal, Alpamayo launch, SpaceX partnership, falling recession odds, earnings approaching — is unique to this moment. If NVDA breaks out and runs to $250, I don't get to buy at $224 with the same catalyst pipeline. That's true.

But the aggressive analyst is making an implicit assumption that they haven't examined: they're assuming the current catalyst pipeline is unambiguously positive. The $2B AI deal is a confirmed revenue catalyst, yes. But the Alpamayo launch and robotaxi expansion are announcements, not revenue. The SpaceX partnership is a partnership, not a contract. And the biggest catalyst — earnings — is a two-sided event where expectations are elevated to the point where a beat-and-raise might not be sufficient. The aggressive analyst treats the catalyst pipeline as a one-way ratchet that can only push the stock higher. Catalysts are events, and events carry risk in both directions. The non-replicable opportunity the aggressive analyst describes is non-replicable in both directions — if earnings disappoint, the opportunity to deploy at $224 before the disappointment is also gone, and the aggressive analyst has 20% of the position deployed at a price that will look expensive in hindsight.

The aggressive analyst also argues that regret in the breakout scenario is "permanent" while regret in the pullback scenario is "temporary." This is circular reasoning. They assume the stock recovers from pullbacks — making pullback regret temporary — but they don't assume the stock could continue declining — which would make pullback regret permanent. The aggressive analyst is building an asymmetry into their regret framework by assuming different recovery dynamics for different scenarios. If the stock pulls back to $206 and then continues to $190, the regret of having deployed 20% at $224 is very permanent. If the stock breaks out to $240 and then pulls back to $224, the regret of having missed the entry is temporary — you can deploy at $224, which is where we are right now. The aggressive analyst's permanent-versus-temporary distinction depends on assumptions about future price trajectories that they can't know in advance. It's not an analytical insight. It's a bull-market bias dressed up as behavioral science.

The neutral analyst's regret-minimization framework is more balanced, but it has its own problem. They calculate that 15% minimizes regret because it creates "modest regret in all scenarios." But regret minimization, when applied to a situation where the expected value is approximately neutral, produces a recommendation to deploy just enough to feel involved without exposing the portfolio to meaningful harm. That's not risk management. It's participation management. The neutral analyst is optimizing for the trader's emotional comfort across scenarios rather than for the portfolio's risk-adjusted return. A trader who deploys 15% because it minimizes regret is not making an investment decision. They're making a psychological adjustment. And while psychology matters in trading — I've acknowledged the behavioral arguments throughout this debate — psychology should not be the primary driver of capital allocation. The primary driver should be the risk-reward profile, and the risk-reward profile at $224 is unfavorable.

The neutral analyst also makes a point that I think deserves more scrutiny. They say my confirmation framework — waiting for two closes above $224.05 on strong volume — means buying at $228-232 instead of $224, and that this is paying more for the same asset. Both colleagues have made this argument, and I want to address it one final time because it's the crux of the disagreement.

The aggressive analyst and the neutral analyst both treat $224 and $230 as the same asset at different prices. They're not. They're the same asset at different information states. At $224, the Bollinger Band is untested, the TD-9 is at negative 6, price is 8.7% above the 50-SMA, and the bear-case PEG is thinner than the plan assumed. At $230, after two confirmed closes above $224.05 on strong volume, the Bollinger Band has been decisively broken, the TD-9 has either completed and been invalidated or reset, and price has demonstrated that buyers are willing to defend the $224 level. These are different risk propositions. The aggressive analyst says confirmation provides "information with a short shelf life" because earnings will provide more information within weeks. But this is backwards. If earnings is the highest-information event and it's weeks away, that's an argument for deploying less now and more after earnings, not for deploying more now on the basis of secondary catalysts and momentum indicators. The aggressive analyst is simultaneously arguing that earnings makes price confirmation irrelevant and that earnings justifies deploying before it happens. You can't have it both ways. Either earnings is the decisive event — in which case you wait for it — or the pre-earnings catalysts are sufficient — in which case you don't need earnings to justify deployment. The aggressive analyst is using each argument to support the other without acknowledging that they point in opposite directions.

Here's my final recommendation, and I want to be specific about why it differs from both colleagues' proposals.

Do not deploy capital at $224. The expected value is negative or approximately neutral — and approximately neutral is not a deployment criterion. The technical extension is at its widest in the lookback period. The TD-9 exhaustion count is three sessions from completion, with all three timeframes counting sell setups. The trader's risk framework is built on a PEG of 0.63 that the aggressive analyst's own calculations show is actually 0.80-0.85, and potentially higher with genuinely conservative inputs. There are unconfirmed supply chain risks with asymmetric downside in a supply chain that has less buffer than normal. And the stock has a 2.215 beta in a market where the 86% no-cut probability is near its ceiling and could shift hawkish.

If NVDA sustains two daily closes above $224.05 on volume exceeding 100 million shares, deploy 15% at whatever price that occurs. The information value justifies the premium. If NVDA pulls back to the $206-212 confluence zone, deploy 50% with the daily SuperTrend stop at $199.78, where the risk-reward is dramatically superior. Reserve 35% for post-earnings deployment, when the information asymmetry is resolved and the path forward is clearer. Size for a 25% drawdown rather than 22%, to account for the beta and the mixed macro signals.

The aggressive analyst asked one final time what we're waiting for. Let me answer as precisely as I can. We're waiting for the TD-9 count to either complete or reset, which happens within three sessions. We're waiting for the Bollinger Band test to resolve — either through a confirmed breakout or a rejection. We're waiting for the 50-SMA extension to normalize from its widest spread in the lookback period. We're waiting for the earnings catalyst that all three analysts agree is the highest-information event. And we're waiting because the neutral analyst's own calculation — which both colleagues are now using to justify deployment — showed that the marginal value of the starter tranche is approximately zero, and deploying capital at approximately zero expected value is not risk management. It's a coin flip with extra steps.

The aggressive analyst says waiting is an active bet on a pullback. The neutral analyst says every position, including cash, is a bet. Both are right. But the cash position has a property that no deployed position has: it preserves optionality. Cash at $224 can become NVDA at $224 if the breakout confirms, NVDA at $210 if the pullback comes, or NVDA at $230 if earnings delivers. Capital deployed at $224 has one outcome: NVDA at $224, for better or worse. In a market where the expected value is approximately neutral, the position that preserves the most optionality is the superior position. And cash preserves more optionality than any deployment.

The aggressive analyst's approach will look brilliant in the breakout scenario. The neutral analyst's approach will look reasonable in most scenarios. My approach will look brilliant in the non-breakout scenarios, which are 80-85% probable by the trader's own framework. But the difference isn't just about which scenario is more likely. It's about the asymmetry of outcomes. If I'm wrong, the cost is opportunity, and opportunity is recoverable. If the aggressive analyst is wrong, the cost is capital, and capital takes months to recover. If the neutral analyst is wrong — deploying 15% at a negative or neutral EV — the cost is small but unnecessary, and unnecessary costs compound over time.

In risk management, the discipline isn't in deploying capital when the expected value is positive. That's easy. The discipline is in NOT deploying capital when the expected value is approximately neutral and the variance is high. That's hard, because it feels like you're missing something. But the something you're missing is a coin flip, and coin flips are not an investment strategy. Patience is not timidity. It's the recognition that the cost of waiting is recoverable, the cost of deploying at extension is not, and when you can't tell the difference between positive and negative expected value — which is what a near-zero EV calculation means — the correct response is to wait until you can.

Neutral Analyst¶

Neutral Analyst: Both of my colleagues have made passionate cases, and both have legitimate points buried inside their more extreme rhetoric. Let me challenge each of them where they've drifted too far from the center, because I think the truth lives in the tension between their arguments.

Starting with the aggressive analyst. The core problem with their argument is that they've taken a 15-20% breakout probability and treated it as the base case. They literally say the breakout is "the higher-probability path." That's just mathematically wrong by the trader's own framework. If there's a 15-20% chance of a breakout, there's an 80-85% chance of something else happening — consolidation, pullback, or rejection. The aggressive analyst is building their entire deployment thesis on the minority scenario and then framing the majority scenario as if it's an afterthought. You can't call something "cheap insurance" and simultaneously claim the insured event is the most likely outcome. If the breakout is the higher-probability path, you don't need insurance — you need full deployment. If it's not, then the insurance framing is honest but the cost-benefit analysis changes considerably. The aggressive analyst is having it both ways, and that's not how probability works.

The aggressive analyst also makes a troubling leap on the fundamentals. The forward P/E of 17.37x and PEG of 0.60 are built on a forward EPS of $12.89, but as the conservative analyst correctly points out, Q1 FY2027 net income included $15.93 billion in non-recurring investment gains. That's not a rounding error — it's roughly 27% of the quarter's net income. The conservative analyst's point here is well-taken, and the aggressive analyst doesn't even acknowledge it. If you strip that out, the normalized forward P/E is probably closer to 22-24x and the PEG closer to 0.80-0.85. Still attractive, yes. But not the "trading at a discount to the S&P 500" narrative the aggressive analyst is selling. The aggressive analyst is presenting the best-case version of the fundamentals as the baseline, which is exactly the kind of optimism that gets people hurt when reality delivers something merely good instead of extraordinary.

On the buyback argument, I think the aggressive analyst is confusing capital return with valuation signaling. Management is buying back stock because they're generating $96.68 billion in annual free cash flow and have a fortress balance sheet with $68 billion in net cash. That's what well-managed companies do with excess capital. But as the conservative analyst notes, academic research consistently shows that buybacks peak when business conditions peak — because that's when cash flow peaks. This is pro-cyclical capital allocation, not contrarian insight. The aggressive analyst's claim that management is "voting with their balance sheet that the stock is undervalued" is an interpretation, not a fact. An equally valid interpretation is that management is returning capital because they don't see better internal deployment opportunities at current scale — which at $215 billion in annual revenue is a legitimate concern. Both interpretations are plausible; the aggressive analyst presents only the bullish one.

And the aggressive analyst's dismissal of the HBM memory shortage is genuinely concerning. If NVIDIA has to cut Rubin Ultra capacity due to memory constraints, that directly impacts the forward revenue projections underpinning the entire valuation case. The aggressive analyst doesn't address this at all — they just skip over it. That's not balanced analysis; that's selective evidence curation. The Japan recession probability at 31% also deserves more than a wave of the hand. Japan is integral to the semiconductor supply chain — Tokyo Electron, Shin-Etsu, JSR — and a Japanese recession could create supply chain disruptions that compound the HBM issue. The aggressive analyst treats these as footnotes when they should be part of the risk calculus.

Now let me challenge the conservative analyst, because their argument has its own significant weaknesses.

The conservative analyst says "wait for confirmation" — either two consecutive closes above $224.05 or a pullback to $206-212. But here's the problem they're not addressing: if NVDA does break out and sustains two closes above $224.05, the entry price on the confirmation tranche is likely $228-232, not $224. The conservative analyst is arguing against deploying at $224 while simultaneously proposing to deploy at a higher price if the breakout confirms. That's not risk management — that's paying more for the same stock because you waited for a signal. The cost of confirmation is a worse entry price, and the conservative analyst doesn't even acknowledge this tradeoff.

The conservative analyst also overstates the fragility of V-shaped recoveries. Yes, V-recoveries without consolidation are statistically more prone to pullbacks. But "more prone to pullbacks" is not the same as "will reverse to the confluence zone." The technical report shows that all three SuperTrend timeframes are in confirmed uptrends, the MACD histogram is still expanding, and RSI has room before overbought. These are not the conditions of an imminent reversal. They're the conditions of a trend that's extended but has fuel. The conservative analyst is treating extension as if it guarantees mean reversion, when the data suggests extension increases the probability of a pullback but doesn't make it certain. There's a meaningful difference between "likely to pull back" and "will pull back to $206-212 before any further upside."

The conservative analyst's "thin air between $211 and $224" argument is also overstated. The 10-EMA is at $211.30 and rising sharply — it surged $11.15 in just five sessions. By the time any pullback materializes, the 10-EMA will likely be at $213-215, providing dynamic support that's closer to current price than the conservative analyst suggests. Moving averages aren't static — they follow price. The conservative analyst is treating them as fixed levels when they're trailing indicators that adapt to the trend.

And the conservative analyst's risk asymmetry argument cuts both ways. They say "if I'm wrong, the cost is missed upside on a 20% tranche." But that's not a small cost. If NVDA breaks out and runs to $236 — the 52-week high — before pulling back, the conservative analyst has missed a 5.4% move on 20% of the position. That's over 1% of total position capital in foregone gains, plus they're now facing the choice of chasing at higher levels or remaining in cash. The conservative analyst frames waiting as "free" because no capital is deployed, but opportunity cost is real cost. In a portfolio context, the question isn't just "what could I lose?" — it's "what am I giving up by not participating?"

The conservative analyst also doesn't adequately address the catalyst pipeline. There's a $2 billion AI deal, the Alpamayo platform launch, a SpaceX partnership, and earnings on the horizon. These are not abstract catalysts — they're active, confirmed events that could drive the stock through the Bollinger Band regardless of what the TD-9 count says. The conservative analyst's framework assumes the technical indicators will dominate the price action, but fundamental catalysts can override technical extension. The aggressive analyst is right about one thing: when the catalyst pipeline is this active, the probability of a pullback to the exact $206-212 zone before any further upside is lower than the conservative analyst suggests.

So where does that leave us? Both analysts are right about some things and wrong about others, and the trader's plan is actually closer to the right answer than either of them.

The aggressive analyst is right that NVDA's fundamentals are extraordinary and that complete inaction carries real opportunity cost. The conservative analyst is right that deploying 20% at the most technically extended point on the chart is aggressive and that the valuation metrics are somewhat inflated by non-recurring items.

The moderate approach — and the one I think the trader should adopt — is to execute the BUY but at a reduced starter tranche size. Instead of 20%, deploy 10% at current levels. Here's why this threads the needle better than either extreme.

A 10% starter tranche still provides meaningful participation in the breakout scenario — half the upside capture of the planned 20%, which is significant. But it halves the capital at risk at the most extended point on the chart. If the stock pulls back to $206-212, the unrealized loss on a 10% tranche is roughly 0.4-0.5% of total position capital — genuinely trivial and easily absorbed. If the stock breaks out and runs to $236, the 10% tranche captures a meaningful portion of that move while the 30% confirmation tranche can be deployed to maximize participation. The remaining 10% that was originally allocated to the starter can be reallocated to the $206-212 pullback tranche, increasing it from 50% to 60%, which improves the average entry price if the pullback materializes.

This restructuring preserves the spirit of the trader's three-tranche barbell while reducing the risk that both analysts correctly identified: deploying too much capital at a technically extended level. The aggressive analyst gets their participation. The conservative analyst gets their risk discipline. And the trader gets a plan that wins across more scenarios than either pure approach.

The stop loss at $199.78 is appropriate for the full position framework, but for the starter tranche specifically, I'd suggest a tighter initial stop at the 10-EMA, currently around $211.30. This limits the downside on the starter tranche to approximately 5.7% rather than 10.8%, and if the 10-EMA is breached, it's an early signal that the short-term momentum has shifted before the daily SuperTrend confirms a trend change. The daily SuperTrend at $199.78 remains the hard stop for any tranches deployed at the $206-212 level, where the risk/reward justifies the wider stop.

On the macro front, both analysts cherry-pick. The aggressive analyst highlights the 8% recession probability while dismissing the 86% no-cut probability. The conservative analyst does the reverse. The balanced read is that the macro environment is neutral for NVDA specifically — low recession risk supports enterprise spending, but higher-for-longer rates create multiple compression risk at a $5.4 trillion market cap. These forces partially offset. NVDA's revenue growth may be somewhat rate-insensitive, but its equity valuation is not. That's an argument for measured deployment rather than aggressive or zero deployment.

The sentiment data cuts both ways too. The bullish news flow and 75/25 StockTwits split are genuinely supportive. But the "world is going to end" quote from one user is a reminder that expectations are elevated heading into earnings. A beat-and-raise might not be enough if the bar is perfection. That's another argument for deploying less now and preserving more capital for the post-earnings environment, where the information asymmetry will be resolved and the entry points will be clearer.

So my recommendation is a modified BUY. Deploy 10% of the intended NVDA position at $224 with a tighter stop at the 10-EMA. Reserve 60% for the $206-212 confluence zone, where the fundamental floor and technical support align. Reserve 30% for post-earnings confirmation — whether that's a breakout above $224.05 sustained or a bounce off the confluence zone. This plan acknowledges the breakout risk that the aggressive analyst correctly identifies, respects the technical extension that the conservative analyst correctly flags, and positions the portfolio to capitalize on NVDA's extraordinary fundamentals without overcommitting at a single price point.

The aggressive analyst's approach is too concentrated in the minority scenario. The conservative analyst's approach is too concentrated in the majority scenario. The moderate approach spreads risk across scenarios — and in a stock with NVDA's beta and catalyst density, scenario diversification is the most sustainable risk management strategy available. Neutral Analyst: Both of my colleagues have now had multiple rounds to make their cases, and I've been listening carefully. What strikes me most is that each of them has, in their latest responses, actually strengthened the opposing side's arguments while trying to advance their own. Let me show you exactly where this has happened and why neither extreme position holds up under balanced scrutiny.

Let me start with the aggressive analyst, because their latest response contains what I think is the most consequential admission in this entire debate. They calculated normalized operating EPS at approximately $7.35 and derived a normalized forward P/E of 20-22x with a PEG of 0.80-0.85. They presented this as evidence that the valuation case "still holds" even after stripping out the one-time gains. But the conservative analyst immediately seized on the real implication: the trader's plan explicitly states the bear case produces a PEG of 0.63. The aggressive analyst just showed that the normalized PEG is 0.80-0.85. That means the bear-case assumption in the trader's own framework is not pessimistic at all — it's using the inflated forward EPS that includes the non-recurring investment gains.

This is not a minor quibble. The trader's entire risk framework is built on the idea that even under deliberately pessimistic bear-case assumptions, the PEG stays well below 1.0, providing a wide margin of safety. If the true bear-case PEG is 0.85 rather than 0.63, the margin of safety isn't wide — it's thin. A PEG of 0.85 is still below 1.0, yes, but it's close enough that any disappointment in growth trajectories, any supply chain disruption affecting Rubin, any competitive pressure from AMD or custom silicon could push it above 1.0 and invalidate the undervaluation thesis entirely. The aggressive analyst is arguing for deployment while simultaneously demonstrating that the risk framework justifying that deployment is compromised. They're asking the trader to deploy capital on the basis of a safety margin that they themselves just showed doesn't exist to the degree claimed.

The aggressive analyst tries to rescue this by pointing out that even at 0.80-0.85, the PEG is still below 1.0 and NVDA trades at only a modest premium to the S&P 500 for a company growing revenue 85%. But this comparison is misleading. The S&P 500 average P/E of ~20x is a blend of companies growing at 5-10% with mature business models. NVDA at 22-24x normalized forward earnings is being valued for continued hypergrowth. If that hypergrowth decelerates — and the law of large numbers makes some deceleration mathematically inevitable at $215B in annual revenue — the multiple compresses not just to the S&P average but potentially below it, because the market will re-rate the growth profile. The aggressive analyst treats the current growth rate as a permanent condition. The conservative analyst treats any deceleration as imminent. The balanced view is that growth will likely decelerate gradually, but the timing and magnitude are uncertain, which is exactly why position sizing matters more than entry timing.

Now let me address the aggressive analyst's probability defense, because they made a sophisticated argument that still contains a critical flaw. They argue that the 80-85% non-breakout scenario includes benign outcomes — consolidation, mild pullbacks to $218-220, sideways grinding that resolves upward. That's true. But they then claim the expected value of the starter tranche is "modestly positive across the full distribution" without actually running the numbers. The conservative analyst did run the numbers and came up with negative 0.38% of total capital. Now, the conservative analyst's probability assignments are their own assumptions, not gospel — they assigned 30% to consolidation, 25% to mild pullback, 15% to moderate pullback, 10% to deeper pullback, and 2.5% to crash. These are reasonable but not definitive. However, the conservative analyst at least attempted the calculation. The aggressive analyst asserted a positive EV without providing one. In risk management, an assertion without calculation is worth less than a calculation with debatable assumptions. The conservative analyst wins this round on methodology, even if their specific numbers are debatable.

But here's where the conservative analyst goes wrong in their EV calculation, and it's important. They assume the starter tranche either captures the full upside of the breakout or sits on losses in the pullback scenarios. They don't account for the optionality value of the starter tranche as a position-building tool. If the stock consolidates at $224 and then breaks out on earnings, the starter tranche isn't just capturing the move from $224 to $240 — it's also providing the psychological and structural anchor that allows the trader to deploy the confirmation tranche with conviction rather than chasing. That optionality value isn't captured in a simple EV calculation. The conservative analyst's math is cleaner than reality.

That said, the conservative analyst's point about the trader's bear-case PEG being compromised is the single most important analytical point in this entire debate, and the aggressive analyst hasn't adequately addressed it. You can't build a deployment framework on a margin of safety that turns out to be thinner than assumed. The trader needs to know that their risk framework has a crack in its foundation before they deploy capital on the basis of it.

Now let me challenge the conservative analyst, because their latest response also contains significant weaknesses.

First, on the confirmation framework. The conservative analyst says buying at $228-232 after two confirmed closes above $224.05 is worth the premium because you're buying "certainty" — a different information state. This sounds sophisticated, but it has a practical problem that neither the conservative analyst nor the aggressive analyst fully addresses. The conservative analyst is recommending deploying 15% at $228-232 on confirmation, 50% at $206-212 on pullback, and 35% post-earnings. But if the breakout confirms at $228-232, the pullback to $206-212 may never come. The conservative analyst's plan assumes both scenarios are available — confirmation at higher prices OR pullback to the confluence zone. But in practice, if the breakout confirms, the next pullback might be to $220-225, not $206-212. The conservative analyst is building a plan that requires the market to provide both an entry at confirmation AND an entry at the confluence zone, when the market is likely to provide only one or the other. That's not risk management — it's wishful structuring.

The aggressive analyst actually made this point well, and the conservative analyst's response — that confirmation changes the probability distribution of outcomes — is true but doesn't address the structural problem. Even with better information at $230, if the stock never pulls back to $206-212, the conservative analyst has deployed only 15% of their intended position and missed the primary accumulation zone they identified. The remaining 85% sits in cash earning nothing while the stock runs. The conservative analyst's framework optimizes for the entry price of each tranche but doesn't optimize for the probability of actually deploying the full position. A plan that leaves you 85% in cash through a multi-month rally because the ideal pullback never came is not a successful risk management strategy — it's an opportunity cost disaster that looks disciplined in theory and underperforms in practice.

Second, the conservative analyst's velocity argument is their strongest technical point but also their most double-edged. They argue that the 18% rally in seven sessions makes the current $224 test more dangerous than June's test because parabolic momentum into resistance is the textbook setup for sharp reversals. This is a legitimate concern. But the aggressive analyst correctly noted that the current momentum profile — MACD at +3.06, histogram still expanding, RSI at 64.42 with room before 70, volume of 158M shares on August 5th — is qualitatively different from June's. The conservative analyst concedes this point but argues velocity trumps momentum strength. The truth is that neither analyst can resolve this tension with certainty because both factors are simultaneously in play. Strong momentum can power through resistance, and parabolic velocity can trigger sharp reversals. These are not mutually exclusive — they're competing forces, and the outcome depends on which force dominates, which we can't know in advance. The balanced response to genuine uncertainty is not to bet entirely on one outcome — which both the aggressive analyst (bet on breakout) and conservative analyst (bet on reversal) are doing — but to position for multiple outcomes.

Third, the conservative analyst makes a nuanced point about the Trade Desk's guidance that deserves more attention than either analyst has given it. They argue that Trade Desk's weakness could signal enterprise budgets are under pressure, with AI capex cannibalizing other spending rather than representing incremental demand. If the pie is shrinking and AI is just taking a bigger slice, the demand pattern is less durable than if the pie is growing. This is a genuinely important distinction that the aggressive analyst glossed over by framing Trade Desk's weakness as evidence of AI budget reallocation. The aggressive analyst assumes the pie is growing. The conservative analyst assumes it might be shrinking. The balanced view is that we don't have enough data to determine which is true, but the Trade Desk signal is at minimum a yellow flag that warrants monitoring. Enterprise IT budgets are not infinitely elastic, and if AI capex is coming at the expense of other spending rather than from incremental budget expansion, the supercycle's duration could be shorter than projected.

Fourth, on the supply chain risks — the HBM shortage and Japan recession. I want to challenge both analysts here. The aggressive analyst dismisses the HBM signal because it's from one StockTwits post with no institutional confirmation. The conservative analyst treats it as a meaningful risk that should inform position sizing. The balanced position is between these two. The aggressive analyst is right that a single retail post doesn't constitute a confirmed supply chain disruption. But the conservative analyst is right that at a $5.4 trillion market cap where the entire growth thesis depends on Rubin execution, you don't need confirmation to take the risk seriously — you need confirmation to dismiss it. The asymmetry of information here matters: the cost of being wrong about HBM supply (Rubin delays, revenue misses, multiple compression) is far greater than the cost of sizing slightly more conservatively while monitoring the situation. The aggressive analyst is applying a binary framework — either it's confirmed and matters, or it's unconfirmed and doesn't. The reality is that unconfirmed risks with asymmetric downside should reduce position sizing, not prevent deployment entirely.

On Japan — the aggressive analyst correctly notes the probability is declining rapidly (down 22.5pp week over week) and that 69% probability of no recession is the base case. The conservative analyst correctly notes that 31% is still nearly one-in-three and that the HBM and Japan risks compound rather than operating in isolation. Both are right. The balanced take: Japan is a declining but still material risk that should be monitored, not a reason to sit in cash, but also not something to wave away. The compounding point is important — if HBM supplies are already tight and a Japanese recession reduces capital expenditure at semiconductor materials companies, the combined impact could be greater than either risk alone. This is a reason for moderate position sizing, not for zero deployment.

Now let me address where I think both analysts have converged on something important without realizing it. The aggressive analyst calculated normalized PEG at 0.80-0.85 and argued this still supports deployment. The conservative analyst used this same calculation to argue the trader's risk framework is compromised. Both are right. The PEG of 0.80-0.85 still suggests NVDA is attractively valued — but not with the wide margin of safety the trader's plan assumed. This means the deployment decision should be more nuanced than either "deploy aggressively because the fundamentals are extraordinary" or "don't deploy because the margin of safety is thin." The right response to a thinner-than-expected margin of safety is to deploy moderately — enough to participate in the upside if the thesis plays out, but not so much that you're overexposed if the thinner margin proves to be inadequate.

This brings me to my recommendation, and I want to be specific about why I'm modifying the trader's plan rather than accepting or rejecting it wholesale.

Deploy 15% of the intended NVDA position at current levels (~$224), not 20% and not 10%. Here's the reasoning. The aggressive analyst's 20% is too large given that the trader's own bear-case PEG assumption appears to be based on inflated earnings — the margin of safety is thinner than the plan assumes, and deploying the full planned starter tranche on a compromised risk framework is imprudent. But the conservative analyst's 0% is too extreme given that even the normalized PEG of 0.80-0.85 is still below the undervaluation threshold, the catalyst pipeline is genuinely active, and all three SuperTrend timeframes are confirmed uptrend. The neutral analyst's earlier suggestion of 10% was a reasonable starting point, but it overcorrects in response to the technical extension without adequately accounting for the catalyst density and the improving macro picture (recession odds down 5pp to 8%).

Fifteen percent splits the difference in a way that's not arbitrary — it's calibrated to the specific risk profile. The starter tranche's expected cost under the bear framework was estimated at 0.85% of total capital at 20% sizing. At 15%, that cost drops to approximately 0.64% of total capital. The conservative analyst's EV calculation showed negative 0.38% at 20% — at 15%, that scales to roughly negative 0.28%. That's a small negative expected value, but it's offset by the optionality value of having a position established if the breakout occurs, which the EV calculation doesn't capture. The 15% sizing acknowledges that the expected value at $224 is probably slightly negative — as the conservative analyst demonstrated — while recognizing that the optionality value of participation justifies accepting a small negative EV in exchange for scenario coverage.

Use the daily SuperTrend at $199.78 as the stop, not the 10-EMA. The aggressive analyst made a compelling operational case here. With NVDA's 2.215 beta and recent daily ranges of $5-7, a 10-EMA stop would likely be triggered by normal volatility before the thesis develops. The daily SuperTrend requires a daily close to confirm, which filters out intraday noise. The wider stop does mean more downside risk per tranche — approximately 10.8% versus 5.7% — but this is accounted for in the reduced tranche size. At 15% of the intended position with a 10.8% stop distance, the maximum loss on the starter tranche is approximately 1.62% of total position capital, which is manageable within the trader's framework of sizing for a 22% drawdown.

Reserve 55% for deployment at the $206-212 confluence zone, up from the trader's planned 50%. The additional 5% comes from the reduction in the starter tranche. This increases the capital available at the optimal entry point identified by both the bull and bear analysts, improving the average entry price if the pullback materializes. If the pullback doesn't materialize, the 15% starter plus the 30% confirmation tranche still provides 45% participation — less than the trader's planned 50%, but the 55% reserved for the confluence zone can be reallocated to the confirmation tranche if the breakout sustains and the pullback zone is never reached.

Reserve 30% for post-earnings confirmation, unchanged from the trader's plan. The earnings catalyst is the highest-conviction information event on the horizon. Deploying 30% after earnings — whether the stock has broken out or pulled back — ensures that the largest tranche is deployed with the most information. Both analysts agree that information asymmetry will be resolved post-earnings. The aggressive analyst treats this as a reason to deploy now and add later. The conservative analyst treats this as a reason to wait. The balanced approach is to deploy a small amount now, reserve the majority for the information-rich post-earnings environment, and maintain a meaningful allocation for the technical confluence zone if it materializes before earnings.

On the macro front, both analysts are cherry-picking. The aggressive analyst highlights the 8% recession probability while dismissing the 86% no-cut probability as "already priced in." The conservative analyst highlights the 86% no-cut probability while noting it could go higher, and dismisses the 8% recession probability. The balanced read is that the macro environment is a mild headwind for NVDA's equity valuation but a neutral-to-positive environment for NVDA's revenue. Low recession risk supports enterprise spending, which supports revenue. Higher-for-longer rates create multiple compression risk at $5.4T market cap, which creates a ceiling on equity upside. These forces partially offset. The net effect is neither strongly bullish nor strongly bearish — it's a macro environment that argues for measured deployment rather than aggressive or zero deployment. The 86% no-cut probability being "already priced in" is the aggressive analyst's strongest macro point — the stock did rally 18% in seven sessions despite this rate environment, which suggests the marginal buyer is fundamentals-driven. But the conservative analyst is right that "priced in" doesn't mean "gone" — if the probability shifts from 86% to 95% due to tariff-driven inflation or fiscal stimulus, that's a negative surprise that isn't reflected in the current price.

On the sentiment data, the balanced read is that sentiment is constructive but not euphoric, with elevated expectations into earnings that create asymmetric risk around the earnings event. A sentiment score of 7.0/10 with medium confidence is moderately bullish — not the 9+ that would signal euphoria. The bullish news flow (5 of 8 headlines bullish, zero bearish) is genuinely supportive. But the "world is going to end" StockTwits post, while from a single user, reflects a broader reality about earnings expectations. The Zacks headline questioning whether to buy after the rally is a tempering signal, as the aggressive analyst notes. The net picture: sentiment supports a constructive posture but argues for keeping powder dry for the post-earnings environment, where the information asymmetry will be resolved and the entry points will be clearer. This is another argument for the 15-55-30 split rather than the trader's 20-50-30 or the conservative analyst's 0-50-35-15.

The aggressive analyst's approach is too aggressive given that the trader's own risk framework is compromised by the inflated earnings issue. The conservative analyst's approach is too conservative given that the fundamentals, even normalized, remain attractive and the catalyst pipeline is genuinely active. The moderate approach — 15% now, 55% for the confluence zone, 30% for post-earnings — acknowledges the real risks both analysts identified while preserving meaningful participation in the upside scenarios. It doesn't optimize for any single outcome. It optimizes for robustness across the full distribution of outcomes, which is the most sustainable approach in a stock with NVDA's beta, catalyst density, and fundamental profile.

Both analysts will likely object to this. The aggressive analyst will say 15% instead of 20% sacrifices meaningful upside for a trivial reduction in risk. The conservative analyst will say 15% instead of 0% deploys capital at a negative expected value point. But the aggressive analyst's objection ignores that the risk framework justifying 20% is compromised — you don't deploy the full planned amount when you discover the margin of safety is thinner than assumed. And the conservative analyst's objection treats a slightly negative EV as if it's disqualifying, when the optionality value of participation — which isn't captured in the EV calculation — justifies accepting a small negative EV in exchange for scenario coverage.

The truth is that neither analyst can know with certainty whether $224 is the right entry or the wrong one. The aggressive analyst is making a bet on momentum and catalysts. The conservative analyst is making a bet on mean reversion and extension. Both bets are defensible. Both bets could be wrong. The approach that survives being wrong is the one that doesn't overcommit to any single thesis — and that's the moderate approach. The aggressive analyst now acknowledges that the normalized PEG is somewhere between 0.70 and 0.85, depending on assumptions about sequential growth continuation. The aggressive analyst is now defending the plan using the second argument, and they don't seem to notice the bait-and-switch.

The conservative analyst caught this, and their point about the PEG denominator is the single most technically correct observation in this entire debate. The aggressive analyst used current sequential quarterly revenue growth — roughly 20% per quarter, annualized — to argue the PEG compresses toward 0.70-0.75. But PEG's denominator is supposed to reflect sustainable forward earnings growth over a multi-year horizon, not current quarterly momentum. At $215 billion in annual revenue, sustaining 85% growth would require adding another $180+ billion next year. The law of large numbers doesn't make this impossible, but it makes it probabilistically unlikely to sustain at current rates. A more realistic forward growth rate of 35-45% — still extraordinary — puts the normalized PEG at roughly 0.75 to 0.88 depending on which normalized P/E you use. The margin of safety exists, but it's meaningfully thinner than the 0.63 the trader's plan assumed.

Here's what the aggressive analyst doesn't grapple with: a thinner margin of safety doesn't mean don't invest. It means invest less at the current price point and preserve more capital for entry points where the risk-reward is clearer. The aggressive analyst treats the thinner margin of safety as a binary — either it's below 1.0 and you deploy, or it's above 1.0 and you don't. But risk management isn't binary. A PEG of 0.63 justifies deploying 20% at the most extended point on the chart because the margin of safety absorbs the execution risk. A PEG of 0.80-0.85 justifies deploying less at that point because the margin of safety is thinner and the execution risk — Rubin delays, competitive pressure, hyperscaler spending normalization — could compress it further. The sizing should scale with the margin of safety, not remain fixed regardless.

The improvement in technical conditions benefits the 50% tranche deployed later at better levels. The aggressive analyst is conflating plan-level value with tranche-level value, and the conservative analyst caught it.

In practice, behavioral finance research is unambiguous: traders who set rules to "wait for confirmation" frequently fail to execute those rules when the confirmation comes, because the emotional cost of buying at a higher price after explicitly waiting for a lower one creates a behavioral barrier. The starter tranche isn't just about participation — it's about commitment devices. The conservative analyst treats this as a psychological crutch. Now let me challenge the conservative analyst, because their latest response also contains a significant flaw that neither the aggressive analyst nor I have fully addressed.

The starter tranche's cost basis is irrelevant if the plan is to hold through the pullback and average down. The conservative analyst is calculating the mark-to-market loss on the starter tranche as if it's a realized loss, when in the context of the three-tranche plan, it's a temporary unrealized loss that gets diluted by subsequent deployments at lower prices.

This matters for the EV calculation. If the stock pulls back to $209 and the trader deploys 50% there, the blended cost basis on 70% of the position is $214. The starter tranche's $224 cost basis doesn't disappear, but it becomes part of a blended position with a much better average entry. The conservative analyst's EV calculation treats the starter tranche as a standalone trade that's marked to market at each outcome. But the trader's plan treats it as the first leg of a cost-averaging strategy. These are fundamentally different frameworks, and the conservative analyst is applying the wrong one.

That said, the conservative analyst's broader point still holds in a modified form. The conservative analyst is paying 2.7% more for the same asset to get confirmation. Sometimes that's worth it. But the conservative analyst treats it as always worth it, which is too rigid.

The conservative analyst's "inspection" analogy — buying a house before vs. after inspection — is clever but incomplete. A house inspection reveals latent defects that are binary and material: foundation cracks, electrical problems, water damage. Technical confirmation in trading is probabilistic, not binary. Two closes above $224.05 on strong volume doesn't reveal a "defect" in the $224 entry — it shifts the probability distribution of outcomes. But the shift isn't from "uncertain" to "certain." It's from "one probability distribution" to "a somewhat better probability distribution." The conservative analyst is overstating the information value of confirmation. It's worth something. It's not worth an unlimited premium.

On the supply chain risks, I want to challenge both analysts one final time. The aggressive analyst's argument that unconfirmed risks shouldn't affect position sizing because they can't be quantified is intellectually lazy. We make sizing decisions under uncertainty every day in risk management. You don't need a precise probability distribution to recognize that an unconfirmed supply chain risk with asymmetric downside — potential Rubin delays at a $5.4T market cap where the growth thesis depends on Rubin execution — warrants a more conservative posture. The aggressive analyst is right that you can't quantify the exact sizing reduction. But that doesn't mean the reduction should be zero. It means the reduction should be modest and proportional to the plausibility of the risk, which is low-to-moderate based on a single retail post with no institutional confirmation.

The conservative analyst's argument that the signals "compound" rather than "add" sounds sophisticated but is actually just an assertion. Compounding requires interaction effects. The conservative analyst hasn't demonstrated interaction effects between the HBM signal, the Japan recession probability, the technical extension, and the PEG normalization issue. They're asserting that these risks multiply without showing the causal pathways. In reality, some of these risks are correlated (HBM and Japan both touch the semiconductor supply chain) and some are independent (PEG normalization is a valuation issue unrelated to supply chain dynamics). Treating all risks as compounding overstates the aggregate risk. Treating all risks as independent understates it. The truth is in between, which is — once again — the moderate position.

Now let me address what I think is the real strategic question that both analysts keep dancing around but neither fully confronts.

The aggressive analyst frames this as: the catalysts are live, the trend is confirmed, deploy now. The conservative analyst frames this as: the technical signals are flashing caution, the risk framework is compromised, wait for better information. Both are partially right. But they're both making implicit assumptions about the time horizon of the trade that they haven't examined.

The trader's plan is a three-tranche accumulation strategy. This is not a single-entry trade. It's a position-building process that unfolds over weeks, potentially through earnings and beyond. The question isn't "should we buy NVDA at $224?" The question is "what is the optimal first step in a multi-step accumulation process?"

From that perspective, both extremes look different. The aggressive analyst's 20% starter is the first step of a process that deploys the remaining 80% at lower prices or on confirmation. The conservative analyst's 0% starter is the first step of a process that deploys 100% at lower prices or on confirmation. The difference isn't 20% vs. 0% — it's the shape of the accumulation curve. The aggressive curve front-loads more capital at current levels. The conservative curve back-loads more capital at future levels.

The optimal accumulation curve depends on the probability distribution of future prices, which neither analyst can know with certainty. But we can say this: the distribution is wider than either analyst assumes. The aggressive analyst's distribution is too concentrated on the upside. The conservative analyst's distribution is too concentrated on the downside. The accumulation curve that performs best across the widest range of distributions is one that deploys modestly at current levels — enough to participate in upside scenarios and provide a behavioral anchor — while reserving the majority of capital for entry points where the risk-reward is clearer. This is neither the aggressive 20-50-30 split nor the conservative 0-50-35-15 split. It's something in between.
